**American Jews** ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): יהודים אמריקאים, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Hebrew): *Yehudim Amerikaim*; [Yiddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish_language): אמעריקאנער אידן, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Yiddish): *Amerikaner Idn*) or **Jewish Americans** are [American](19792942.md) citizens who are [Jewish](25955086.md), whether by ethnicity, [religion](15624.md), or [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture).[^cite_note-4] According to a 2020 poll conducted by [Pew Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research), approximately two thirds of American Jews identify as [Ashkenazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi), 3% identify as [Sephardic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic), and 1% identify as [Mizrahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews). An additional 6% identify as some combination of the three categories, and 25% do not identify as any particular category.[^cite_note-pew-2020-5]

During the colonial era, Sephardic Jews who arrived via [Portugal](23033.md) and via Brazil ([Dutch Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Brazil))[^cite_note-6] represented the bulk of America's then small Jewish population. While their descendants are a minority nowadays, they represent the remainder of those original American Jews along with an array of other Jewish communities, including more recent Sephardi Jews, Mizrahi Jews, [Beta Israel-Ethiopian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_Israel), [various other Jewish ethnic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions), as well as a smaller number of [gerim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerim) (converts). The American Jewish community manifests a wide range of Jewish cultural traditions, encompassing the full spectrum of [Jewish religious observance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_movements).

Depending on religious definitions and varying population data, the United States has the largest or second largest Jewish community in the world, after [Israel](9282173.md). As of 2020, the American Jewish population is estimated at 7.5 million people, accounting for 2.4% of the total US population. This includes 4.2 million adults who identify their religion as Jewish, 1.5 million Jewish adults who identify with no religion, and 1.8 million Jewish children.[^cite_note-Mitchell-2021-1] It is estimated that up to 15 million Americans are part of the "enlarged" American Jewish population, accounting for 4.5% of the total US population, consisting of those who have [at least one Jewish grandparent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Who_is_a_Jew%3F) and would be eligible for [Israeli citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_citizenship) under the [Law of Return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Return).[^cite_note-7]

## History

Main article: [History of the Jews in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_United_States)

Further information: [Antisemitism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_the_United_States) and [History of antisemitism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_antisemitism_in_the_United_States)

### Colonial era

Further information: [Spanish-Portuguese Jews in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish-Portuguese_Jews_in_the_United_States)

Jews were present in the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies) since the mid-17th century.[^cite_note-8][^cite_note-9] However, they were few in number, with at most 200 to 300 having arrived by 1700.[^cite_note-Atkin-p305-10] Those early arrivals were mostly [Sephardi Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews) immigrants, of Western Sephardic (also known as [Spanish and Portuguese Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_and_Portuguese_Jews)) ancestry,[^cite_note-11] but by 1720, [Ashkenazi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews) from [diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora) communities in Central and Eastern Europe predominated.[^cite_note-Atkin-p305-10]

For the first time, the English [Plantation Act 1740](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation_Act_1740) permitted Jews to become British citizens and emigrate to [the colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_America). The first famous Jew in US history was [Chaim Salomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaim_Salomon), a Polish-born Jew who emigrated to New York and played an important role in the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution). He was a successful financier who supported the patriotic cause and helped raise most of the money needed to finance the American Revolution.[^cite_note-12]

### 1800s

Despite some of them being denied the right to vote or hold office in local jurisdictions, Sephardi Jews became active in community affairs in the 1790s, after they were granted political equality in the five states where they were most numerous.[^cite_note-13] Until about 1830, [Charleston, South Carolina had more Jews than anywhere else in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Charleston,_South_Carolina). Large-scale Jewish immigration commenced in the 19th century, when, by mid-century, many [German Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Germany) had arrived, migrating to the United States in large numbers due to antisemitic laws and restrictions in their countries of birth.[^cite_note-14] They primarily became merchants and shop-owners. Gradually early Jewish arrivals from the east coast would travel westward, and in the fall of 1819 the first Jewish religious services west of the Appalachian Range were conducted during the [High Holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Holidays) in [Cincinnati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati), the oldest Jewish community in the Midwest. Gradually the [Cincinnati Jewish community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Cincinnati) would adopt novel practices under the leadership Rabbi [Isaac Meyer Wise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Meyer_Wise), the father of Reform Judaism in the United States,[^cite_note-15] such as the inclusion of women in *[minyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minyan)*.[^cite_note-16] A large community grew in the region with the arrival of [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Jews) and [Lithuanian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_Jews) in the latter half of the 1800s, leading to the establishment of [Manischewitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manischewitz), one of the largest producers of American kosher products and now based in [New Jersey](21648.md), and the oldest continuously published Jewish newspaper in the United States, and second-oldest continuous published in the world, *[The American Israelite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_American_Israelite)*, established in 1854 and still extant in Cincinnati.[^cite_note-17] By 1880 there were approximately 250,000 Jews in the United States, many of them being the educated, and largely secular, German Jews, although a minority population of the older [Sephardi Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews) families remained influential.



[Eastern European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) Jewish immigrants arriving in New York

Jewish migration to the United States increased dramatically in the early 1880s, as a result of persecution and economic difficulties in parts of Eastern Europe. Most of these new immigrants were [Yiddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish_language)-speaking Ashkenazi Jews, most of whom arrived from poor diaspora communities of the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Russia) and the [Pale of Settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pale_of_Settlement), located in modern-day [Poland](22936.md), [Lithuania](17675.md), [Belarus](3457.md), [Ukraine](31750.md), and [Moldova](19260.md). During the same period, great numbers of Ashkenazic Jews also arrived from [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia_\(Eastern_Europe\)), at that time the most impoverished region of the [Austro-Hungarian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria-Hungary) with a heavy Jewish urban population, driven out mainly by economic reasons. Many Jews also emigrated from [Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Romania). Over 2,000,000 Jews landed between the late 19th century and 1924 when the [Immigration Act of 1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1924) restricted immigration. Most settled in the [New York metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_metropolitan_area), establishing the world's major concentrations of the Jewish population. In 1915, the circulation of the daily [Yiddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish) newspapers was half a million in New York City alone, and 600,000 nationally. In addition, thousands more subscribed to the numerous weekly papers and the many magazines in Yiddish.[^cite_note-19]

### 1900s

At the beginning of the 1900s, these newly arrived Jews built support networks consisting of many small [synagogues](48559.md) and *[Landsmanshaften](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landsmanshaft)* (German and Yiddish for "Countryman Associations") for Jews from the same town or village. American Jewish writers of the time urged [assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_assimilation) and integration into the wider [American culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_States), and Jews quickly became part of American life. Approximately 500,000 American Jews (or half of all Jewish males between 18 and 50) fought in [World War II](32927.md), and after the war younger families joined the new trend of [suburbanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburbanization). There, Jews became increasingly assimilated and demonstrated rising [intermarriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfaith_marriage). The suburbs facilitated the formation of new centers, as Jewish school enrollment more than doubled between the end of World War II and the mid-1950s, while synagogue affiliation jumped from 20% in 1930 to 60% in 1960; the fastest growth came in Reform and, especially, Conservative congregations.[^cite_note-20] More recent waves of Jewish emigration from Russia and other regions have largely joined the mainstream American Jewish community.

Americans of Jewish descent have been successful in many fields and aspects over the years.[^cite_note-21][^cite_note-22] The Jewish community in America has gone from being part of the [lower class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_lower_class) of society, with numerous employments barred to them,[^cite_note-23] to being a group with a high concentrations in members of the academia and a per capita income higher than the average in the United States.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25][^cite_note-26]

| < \$30,000 | \$30,000–49,999 | \$50,000–99,999 | \$100,000+ |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 16% | 15% | 24% | 44% |

### Self-identity

Scholars debate whether the historical experience of Jews in the United States has been such a unique experience as to validate [American exceptionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_exceptionalism).[^cite_note-28] Korelitz (1996) shows how American Jews during the late 19th and early 20th centuries abandoned a [racial definition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_identity) of Jewishness in favor of one that embraced ethnicity. The key to understanding this transition from a racial self-definition to a cultural or ethnic one can be found in the *[Menorah Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menorah_Journal)* between 1915 and 1925. During this time contributors to the Menorah promoted a [cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture), rather than a racial, religious, or other views of Jewishness as a means to define Jews in a world that threatened to overwhelm and absorb Jewish uniqueness. The journal represented the ideals of the menorah movement established by [Horace M. Kallen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horace_M._Kallen) and others to promote a revival in Jewish cultural identity and combat the idea of race as a means to define or identify peoples.[^cite_note-29]

Siporin (1990) states that the family [folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folklore) of [ethnic Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_Jews) provides insights that illuminate collective history and transform it into art, and that these insights tell us how Jews have survived being uprooted and transformed. Many immigrant narratives bear a theme of the arbitrary nature of fate and the reduced state of immigrants in a new culture. By contrast, ethnic family narratives tend to show the ethnicity more in charge of his life, and perhaps in danger of losing his Jewishness altogether. Some stories show how a family member successfully negotiated the conflict between ethnic and American identities.[^cite_note-30] After 1960, memories of [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust), together with the [Six-Day War](29328.md) in 1967 had major impacts on fashioning Jewish [ethnic identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_identity). Some have argued that the Holocaust highlighted for Jews the importance of their ethnic identity at a time when other minorities were asserting their own.[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33] The world's largest Jewish gathering outside of Israel occurred in [Edison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edison,_New_Jersey), central New Jersey, on December 1, 2024.[^cite_note-34]

### The Holocaust

During the [World War II](32927.md) period, the American Jewish community was bitterly and deeply divided and as a result, it was unable to form a united front. Most Jews who had previously emigrated to the United States from Eastern Europe supported [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism), because they believed that a return to their ancestral homeland was the only solution to the [persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Jews) and the [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) which were then occurring across Europe. One important development was the sudden conversion of many American Jewish leaders to Zionism late in the war.[^cite_note-35] [The Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) was largely ignored by American media as it was happening. Reporters and editors largely did not believe the stories of atrocities which were coming out of Europe.[^cite_note-36]

[The Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) had a profound impact on the Jewish community in the United States, especially after 1960 as Holocaust education improved, as Jews tried to comprehend what had happened during it, and especially as they tried to commemorate it and grapple with it when they looked to the future. [Abraham Joshua Heschel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Joshua_Heschel) summarized this dilemma when he attempted to understand [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp): "To try to answer is to commit a supreme blasphemy. Israel enables us to bear the agony of Auschwitz without radical despair, to sense a ray \[of] God's radiance in the jungles of history."[^cite_note-37]

### Participation in civil rights movements

See also: [Jews in the civil rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_in_the_civil_rights_movement)

Members of the American Jewish community have included prominent participants in [civil rights](37071.md) movements. In the mid-20th century, there were American Jews who were among the most active participants in the [Civil Rights Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Movement) and [feminist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminist) movements. A number of American Jews have also been active figures in the struggle for [gay rights in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_the_United_States).

[Joachim Prinz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joachim_Prinz), president of the [American Jewish Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Jewish_Congress), stated the following when he spoke from the podium at the Lincoln Memorial during the famous [March on Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Washington_for_Jobs_and_Freedom) on August 28, 1963: "As Jews we bring to this great demonstration, in which thousands of us proudly participate, a twofold experience—one of the spirit and one of our history. ... From our Jewish historic experience of three and a half thousand years we say: Our ancient history began with [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery) and the yearning for freedom. During the Middle Ages my people lived for a thousand years in the ghettos of Europe. ... It is for these reasons that it is not merely sympathy and compassion for the black people of America that motivates us. It is, above all and beyond all such sympathies and emotions, a sense of complete identification and solidarity born of our own painful historic experience."[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39]

## Demographics

Further information: [Jews in New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_in_New_York_City)



Jewish Americans by state according to the American Jewish Yearbook, 2020 and the US Census Bureau

As of 2020, the American Jewish population is, [depending on the method of identification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_population_by_country), either the largest in the world, or the second-largest in the world (after [Israel](9282173.md)). Precise population figures vary depending on whether Jews are accounted for based on [halakhic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakha) considerations, or secular, [political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Return) and [ancestral identification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Who_is_a_Jew%3F#Other_non-religious_definitions) factors. There were about four million adherents of Judaism in the US as of 2001, approximately 1.4% of the US population. According to the [Jewish Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Agency), for the year 2023 Israel was home to 7.2 million Jews (46% of the world's Jewish population), while the United States contained 6.3 million (40.1%).[^cite_note-40]

According to [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup,_Inc.) and [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) findings, "at maximum 2.2% of the US adult population has some basis for Jewish self-identification."[^cite_note-41] In 2020, the demographers [Arnold Dashefsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnold_Dashefsky) and Ira M. Sheskin estimated in the [American Jewish Yearbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Jewish_Yearbook) that the American Jewish population totaled 7.15 million, making up 2.17% of the country's 329.5 million inhabitants.[^cite_note-Jewish_American_Population-42][^cite_note-43] In the same year, the American Jewish population was estimated at 7.6 million people, accounting for 2.4% of the total US population, by other organization. This includes 4.9 million adults who identify their religion as Jewish, 1.2 million Jewish adults who identify with no religion, and 1.6 million Jewish children.[^cite_note-44]

According to a 2020 study by the Pew Research Center, the core American Jewish population is estimated at 7.5 million people, this includes 5.8 million Jewish adults.[^cite_note-45] The study found that the median age of the American Jewish population is 49, and around 18% of American Jewish are under the age of 30, while 49% of American Jewish are ages 50 and older.[^cite_note-Pew-2021a-46] The study found also that 9% of American Jewish identify as [LGBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT).[^cite_note-Pew-2021a-46]

The *American Jewish Yearbook population survey* had placed the number of American Jews at 6.4 million, or approximately 2.1% of the total population. This figure is significantly higher than the previous large scale survey estimate, conducted by the 2000–2001 National Jewish Population estimates, which estimated 5.2 million Jews. A 2007 study released by the [Steinhardt Social Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steinhardt_Social_Research_Institute) (SSRI) at [Brandeis University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brandeis_University) presents evidence to suggest that both these figures may be underestimations with a potential 7.0–7.4 million Americans of Jewish descent.[^cite_note-47] Those higher estimates were however arrived at by including all non-Jewish family members and household members, rather than surveyed individuals.[^cite_note-Sergio_DellaPergola_2012._pp._212–283-48] In a 2019 study by [Jews of Color Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_of_Color_Initiative) it was found that approximately 12-15% of Jews in the United States, about 1,000,000 of 7,200,000 identify as multiracial and [Jews of color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_of_color).[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53]

The overall population of Americans of Jewish descent is demographically characterized by an aging population composition and low fertility rates significantly below generational replacement.[^cite_note-Sergio_DellaPergola_2012._pp._212–283-48] However, the [Orthodox Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Jewish) population, concentrated in the [Northeastern United States](431669.md), has fertility rates when taken alone which are significantly higher than both generational replacement and that of the average U.S. population.

The [National Jewish Population Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Jewish_Population_Survey) of 1990 asked 4.5 million adult Jews to identify their denomination. The national total showed 38% were affiliated with the [Reform tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism), 35% were [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Judaism), 6% were [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism), 1% were [Reconstructionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstructionist_Judaism), 10% linked themselves to some other tradition, and 10% said they are "just Jewish."[^cite_note-54] In 2013, Pew Research's Jewish population survey found that 35% of American Jews identified as Reform, 18% as Conservative, 10% as Orthodox, 6% who identified with other sects, and 30% did not identify with a denomination.[^cite_note-55] Pew's 2020 poll found that 37% affiliated with Reform Judaism, 17% with Conservative Judaism, and 9% with Orthodox Judaism. Young Jews are more likely to identify as Orthodox or as unaffiliated compared to older members of the Jewish community.[^cite_note-pew-2020-5]

Many Jews are concentrated in the Northeastern United States, particularly around [New York City](645042.md). The world's largest [menorah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanukkah_menorah) is lit annually at [Grand Army Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Army_Plaza,_Manhattan) in [Manhattan](45470.md), while the largest menorah in [New Jersey](21648.md) is similarly celebrated in [Monroe Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_Township,_Middlesex_County,_New_Jersey), [Middlesex County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middlesex_County,_New_Jersey). Many Jews also live in [South Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Florida), [Los Angeles](18110.md), and other large metropolitan areas, like [Chicago](6886.md), [San Francisco](49728.md), or [Atlanta](3138.md). The metropolitan areas of New York City, Los Angeles, and Miami contain nearly one quarter of the world's Jews[^cite_note-metroareas-56] and the New York City metropolitan area itself contains around a quarter of all Jews living in the United States.

According to a 2025 study of Jewish Life in North America, some 22% of US Jews live in small communities, defined as Jewish communities with fewer than 5,000 Jews living within five miles of their ZIP code. The survey, conducted by the [Jewish Federations of North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Federations_of_North_America), found no statistically significant difference in the antisemitism experienced by Jews in smaller and larger communities, but it found that Jews in small communities are more likely to feel that antisemitism is invalidated or dismissed—58 percent as compared with 48 percent overall.[^cite_note-57]

The [New York City metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_metropolitan_area) is the second-largest Jewish population center in the world after the [Tel Aviv metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv_metropolitan_area) in Israel.[^cite_note-metroareas-56] Several other major cities have large Jewish communities, including [Los Angeles](18110.md), [Miami](53846.md), [Baltimore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Baltimore), [Boston](24437894.md), [Chicago](6886.md), [San Francisco](49728.md) and [Philadelphia](50585.md).[^cite_note-60] In many metropolitan areas, the majority of Jewish families live in suburban areas. The [Greater Phoenix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Phoenix) area was home to about 83,000 Jews in 2002, and has been rapidly growing.[^cite_note-61] The greatest Jewish population on a per-capita basis for incorporated areas in the US are [Kiryas Joel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiryas_Joel) Village, New York (greater than 93% based on language spoken in home),[^cite_note-62] City of [Beverly Hills, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beverly_Hills,_California) (61%),[^cite_note-63] and [Lakewood Township](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakewood_Township,_New_Jersey), New Jersey (59%),[^cite_note-64] with two of the incorporated areas, Kiryas Joel and Lakewood, having a high concentration of Haredi Jews, and one incorporated area, Beverly Hills, having a high concentration of non-Orthodox Jews.

The phenomenon of Israeli migration to the US is often termed *[Yerida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yerida)*. The [Israeli immigrant community in America](19995589.md) is less widespread. The significant Israeli immigrant communities in the United States are in the New York City metropolitan area, Los Angeles, Miami, and Chicago.[^cite_note-65]

- The [Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development) calculated an 'expatriate rate' of 2.9 persons per thousand, putting Israel in the mid-range of expatriate rates among the 175 [OECD](33853117.md) countries examined in 2005.

  [^cite_note-66]

According to the 2001 undertaking[^cite_note-67] of the [National Jewish Population Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Jewish_Population_Survey), 4.3 million American Jews have some sort of strong connection to the Jewish community, whether religious or cultural.

### Assimilation and population changes

These parallel themes have facilitated the extraordinary economic, political, and social success of the American Jewish community, but also have contributed to widespread [cultural assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation).[^cite_note-69] More recently however, the propriety and degree of [assimilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_assimilation) has also become a significant and controversial issue within the modern American Jewish community, with both [political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) and religious skeptics.[^cite_note-70]

While not all Jews disapprove of [intermarriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interreligious_marriage), many members of the Jewish community have become concerned that the high rate of [interfaith marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfaith_marriage_in_Judaism) will result in the eventual disappearance of the American Jewish community. Intermarriage rates have risen from roughly 6% in 1950 and 25% in 1974,[^cite_note-71] to approximately 40–50% in the year 2000.[^cite_note-72] By 2013, the intermarriage rate had risen to 71% for non-Orthodox Jews.[^cite_note-73] This, in combination with the comparatively low birthrate in the Jewish community, has led to a 5% decline in the Jewish population of the United States in the 1990s. In addition to this, when compared with the general American population, the American Jewish community is slightly older.

A third of intermarried couples provide their children with a Jewish upbringing, and doing so is more common among intermarried families raising their children in areas with high Jewish populations.[^cite_note-74] The Boston area, for example, is exceptional in that an estimated 60% of children of intermarriages are being raised Jewish, meaning that intermarriage would actually be contributing to a net *increase* in the number of Jews.[^cite_note-75] As well, some children raised through intermarriage [rediscover and embrace their Jewish roots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baal_teshuva_movement) when they themselves marry and have children.

In contrast to the ongoing trends of assimilation, some communities within American Jewry, such as [Orthodox Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism), have significantly higher birth rates and lower intermarriage rates, and are growing rapidly. The proportion of Jewish synagogue members who were Orthodox rose from 11% in 1971 to 21% in 2000, while the overall Jewish community declined in number. [^cite_note-76] In 2000, there were 360,000 so-called "ultra-orthodox" ([Haredi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haredi)) Jews in USA (7.2%).[^cite_note-manchester-77] The figure for 2006 is estimated at 468,000 (9.4%).[^cite_note-manchester-77] Data from the Pew Center shows that, as of 2013, 27% of American Jews under the age of 18 live in Orthodox households, a dramatic increase from Jews aged 18 to 29, only 11% of whom are Orthodox. The UJA-Federation of New York reports that 60% of Jewish children in the New York City area live in Orthodox homes. In addition to economizing and sharing, many Haredi communities depend on government aid to support their high birth rate and large families. The Hasidic village of [New Square, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Square,_New_York) receives Section 8 housing subsidies at a higher rate than the rest of the region, and half of the population in the Hasidic village of [Kiryas Joel, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiryas_Joel,_New_York) receive food stamps, while a third receive Medicaid.[^cite_note-78]

About half of the American Jews are considered to be religious. Out of this 2,831,000 religious Jewish population, 92% are [non-Hispanic white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_whites), 5% [Hispanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic) (Most commonly from Argentina, Venezuela, or Cuba), 1% [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_people), 1% [black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people) and 1% Other (mixed-race etc.). Almost this many non-religious Jews exist in the United States.[^cite_note-79]

### Race and ethnicity



[Jewish ethnic divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions)

|  | This section may **require [cleanup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Cleanup)** to meet Wikipedia's [quality standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Manual_of_Style). The specific problem is: **duplicated in [American Jews#Jews of European descent](#Jews_of_European_descent).** Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/American_Jews) if you can. *(November 2025)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

The [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) classifies most American Jews as [white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans).[^cite_note-80] Jewish people are culturally diverse and may be of any race, ethnicity, or national origin. Many Jews have culturally assimilated into and are phenotypically indistinguishable from the dominant local populations of regions like [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions#Europe), [the Caucasus and the Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions#The_Caucasus_and_the_Crimea), [North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions#North_Africa), [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions#West_Asia), [Sub-Saharan Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions#Sub-Saharan_Africa), [South, East, and Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions#South,_East,_and_Central_Asia), and the [Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ethnic_divisions#Americas) where they have lived for many centuries.[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82][^cite_note-83] Many American Jews identify themselves as being both Jewish and [white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_people), while many solely identify as Jewish, resisting this identification.[^cite_note-84] Several commentators have observed that "many American Jews retain a feeling of ambivalence about [whiteness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans)".[^cite_note-85] Karen Brodkin explains this ambivalence as rooted in anxieties about the potential loss of [Jewish identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_identity), especially outside of intellectual elites.[^cite_note-Brodkin-1998-86] Similarly, Kenneth Marcus observes a number of ambivalent cultural phenomena which have also been noted by other scholars, and he concludes that "the veneer of whiteness has not established conclusively the racial construction of American Jews".[^cite_note-Marcus-2010-87] The relationship between Jewish identity and white majority identity continues to be described as "complicated" for many American Jews, particularly Ashkenazi and Sephardi Jews of European descent. The issue of Jewish whiteness may be different for many Mizrahi, Sephardi, Black, Asian, and Latino Jews, many of whom may never be considered white by society.[^cite_note-Schechter-2016-88] Many American [white nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism) and [white supremacists](33178.md) view all Jews as non-white, even if they are of European descent.[^cite_note-Andersen-2015-89] Some white nationalists believe that Jews can be white and a small number of white nationalists are [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_in_Jewish_communities).[^cite_note-90]

In 2013, the Pew Research Center's *Portrait of Jewish Americans* found that more than 90% of Jews who responded to its survey described themselves as being [non-Hispanic whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_whites), 2% described themselves as being [black](2154.md), 3% described themselves as being [Hispanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans), and 2% described themselves as having other racial or ethnic backgrounds.[^cite_note-91]

#### Jews by race, ancestry, or national origin

##### Asian American Jews

According to the Pew Research Center, fewer than 1% of American Jews in 2020 identified as [Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans). Around 1% of religious Jews identified as Asian American.[^cite_note-Pew-2021-92]

A small but growing community of around 350 [Indian American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_American) [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_India) lives in the New York City metropolitan area, in both New York state and New Jersey. Many are members of India's [Bene Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bene_Israel) community.[^cite_note-93] The Indian Jewish Congregation of USA, headquartered in New York City, is the center of the organized community.[^cite_note-94]

##### Jews of European descent

|  | This section may **require [cleanup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Cleanup)** to meet Wikipedia's [quality standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Manual_of_Style). The specific problem is: **duplicated in [American Jews#Race and ethnicity](#Race_and_ethnicity).** Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/American_Jews) if you can. *(November 2025)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

&#x20;Jews of European descent are classified as white by the US census and have generally been classified as legally white throughout American history as pertaining to courts, naturalization law, and censuses.[^cite_note-Yang-2016-95] However, Jews long struggled with social equality and had an ambivalent relationship to being part of a white majority in America.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98][^cite_note-Brodkin-1998-86][^cite_note-99] Many American Jews of [European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Europe) descent identify themselves as being both Jewish and [white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_people), while others solely identify themselves as being [Jewish](25955086.md) or identify as both Jewish and non-white.[^cite_note-100] However, Jews of European descent rarely identify as [Jews of color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_of_color). According to the Pew Research Center, the majority of American Jews are non-Hispanic white Ashkenazi Jews.[^cite_note-Pew-2021-92] Law professor [David Bernstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Bernstein_\(law_professor\)) has questioned the idea that American Jews were once considered non-white, writing that American Jews were "indeed considered white by law and by custom" despite the fact that they experienced "discrimination, hostility, assertions of inferiority and occasionally even violence." Bernstein notes that Jews were not targeted by laws against interracial marriage, were allowed to attend whites-only schools, and were classified as white in the Jim Crow South.[^cite_note-101] The sociologists Philip Q. Yang and Kavitha Koshy have also questioned what they call the "becoming white thesis", noting that most Jews of European descent have been legally classified as white since the first US census in [1790](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1790_United_States_census), were legally white for the purposes of the [Naturalization Act of 1790](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalization_Act_of_1790) that limited citizenship to "free White person(s)", and that they could find no legislative or judicial evidence that American Jews had ever been considered non-white.[^cite_note-Yang-2016-95]

Several commentators have observed that "many American Jews retain a feeling of ambivalence about [whiteness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans)".[^cite_note-102] Karen Brodkin explains this ambivalence as rooted in anxieties about the potential loss of [Jewish identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_identity), especially outside of intellectual elites.[^cite_note-Brodkin-1998-86] Similarly, Kenneth Marcus observes a number of ambivalent cultural phenomena which have also been noted by other scholars, and he concludes that "the veneer of whiteness has not established conclusively the racial construction of American Jews".[^cite_note-Marcus-2010-87] The relationship between American Jews and white majority identity continues to be described as "complicated".[^cite_note-Schechter-2016-88] Many American [white nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism) view Jews as non-white.[^cite_note-Andersen-2015-89]

##### Jews of Middle Eastern and North African descent

Jews of Middle Eastern and North African descent (often referred to as [Mizrahi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews)) are classified as white by the US census. Mizrahi Jews sometimes identify as Jews of color, but often do not, and they may or may not be considered people of color by society. Syrian Jews rarely identify as Jews of color. Many Syrian Jews identify as white, Middle Eastern, or otherwise non-white rather than as Jews of color.[^cite_note-Pew-2021-92]

##### African American Jews

Main article: [African-American Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_Jews)

See also: [African American–Jewish relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American%E2%80%93Jewish_relations)

The American Jewish community includes African American Jews and other American [Jews who are of African descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_Jews), a definition which excludes [North African Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghrebi_Jews) Americans, who are currently classified by the US census as being [white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_American) (although a new category was recommended by the Census Bureau for the 2020 census).[^cite_note-103] Estimates of the number of American Jews of African descent in the United States range from 20,000[^cite_note-104] to 200,000.[^cite_note-105] Jews of African descent belong to all American [Jewish denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_denominations). Like their other Jewish counterparts, some black Jews are [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_atheist).

Notable African American Jews include [Drake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drake_\(musician\)), [Lenny Kravitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenny_Kravitz), [Lisa Bonet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Bonet), [Sammy Davis Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Davis_Jr.), [Rashida Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashida_Jones), [Ros Gold-Onwude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ros_Gold-Onwude), [Yaphet Kotto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaphet_Kotto), [Jordan Farmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_Farmar), [Taylor Mays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taylor_Mays), [Daveed Diggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daveed_Diggs), [Alicia Garza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alicia_Garza), [Tiffany Haddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiffany_Haddish), and rabbis [Capers Funnye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capers_Funnye) and [Alysa Stanton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alysa_Stanton).

##### Hispanic and Latin American Jews

Hispanic Jews have lived in what is now the United States since colonial times. The earliest Hispanic Jewish settlers were Sephardi Jews from Spain and Portugal. Beginning in the 1500s, some of the Spanish settlers in what is now [New Mexico](21649.md) and [Texas](29810.md) were [Crypto-Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crypto-Jews), but there was no organized Jewish presence.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107] Later waves of Sephardi immigration brought [Judeo-Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judeo-Spanish) speaking Jews from the Ottoman Empire, in what is now Greece, Turkey, Bulgaria, Libya, and Syria. These Spanish-speaking Sephardi Jews, as well as Sephardi Jews of European descent, such as the [Spanish and Portuguese Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_and_Portuguese_Jews), are sometimes considered culturally but not ethnically Hispanic.

Hispanic and Latin American Jews, particularly Hispanic and Latin American Ashkenazi Jews, often identify as white rather than as Jews of color. Some Jews with roots in Latin America may not identify as "Hispanic" or "Latino" at all, usually due to their recent European immigrant origins.[^cite_note-Pew-2021-92] American Jews of Argentine, Brazilian, and Mexican descent are often Ashkenazi, but some are Sephardi.[^cite_note-108]

#### Jews divided by cultural or Jewish ethnic division groupings

| Ancestry | Population | % of US population |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Ashkenazim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews)[^cite_note-109] | 5,000,000–6,000,000 | 1.8–2.1% |
| [Sephardim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews)[^cite_note-110] | 300,000 | 0.086–0.086% |
| [Mizrahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews) | 250,000 | 0.071–0.071% |
| [Italkim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italqim) | 200,000 | 0.057–0.057% |
| [Bukharim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukharan_Jews) | 50,000–60,000 | 0.014–0.017% |
| [Juhurim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_Jews) | 10,000–40,000 | 0.003–0.011% |

| Total[^cite_note-112] | 5,700,000–8,000,000 | 1.6–2.3% |

A majority of the Jewish population in the United States are [Ashkenazi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews) who descend from [diaspora Jewish populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora) of [Central](5188.md) and [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe). Most American Ashkenazi Jews are non-Hispanic whites, but a minority are Jews of color, Hispanic/Latino, or both.

Largely [expelled from the Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_Jews_from_Spain) in the late 15th century, [Sephardi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews) carried a distinctive [Jewish diasporic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora) identity with them to the [Americas](29833.md) (although in smaller numbers compared to the [Ashkenazi Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jewish) diaspora) and all other places of their exiled settlement. They sometimes settled near existing Jewish communities, such as the one from former [Kurdistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurdistan), or were the first in new frontiers, with their furthest reach via the [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road).[^cite_note-113] As a result of the more recent [Jewish exodus from Arab lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_exodus_from_Arab_lands), many of the Sephardim Tehorim from Western Asia and North Africa relocated to either [Israel](9282173.md) or France, where they form a significant portion of the Jewish communities today. Other significant communities of Sephardim Tehorim also migrated in more recent times from the Near East to [New York City](645042.md), [Argentina](18951905.md), Costa Rica, [Mexico](3966054.md), [Montreal](7954681.md), [Gibraltar](7607314.md), [Puerto Rico](23041.md), and [Dominican Republic](8060.md).[^cite_note-114] Because of poverty and turmoil in Latin America, another wave of Sephardic Jews joined other Latin Americans who migrated to the US, Canada, Spain, and other countries of Europe.

Post-1948, [Mizrahi Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews), mostly thousands from Lebanese, Syrian and Egyptian Jewish descent, as well as some from other [Middle East and North African](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_and_North_Africa) Jewish communities migrated to the United States.

Since the 1990s, around 1000 Hebrew-speaking, [Ethiopian Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Jews) that had settled in Israel as [Ethiopian Jews in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Jews_in_Israel) re-settled in the United States as [Ethiopian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Americans), with around half of the [Ethiopian Jewish Israeli-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Americans#Ethiopian_Jews_in_the_United_States) community living in New York.[^cite_note-115]

### Socioeconomics

See also: [Jewish-American working class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish-American_working_class) and [Model minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Model_minority)

[Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_education) plays a major role as a part of Jewish identity. As Jewish culture puts a special premium on it and stresses the importance of cultivation of intellectual pursuits, scholarship, and learning, American Jews as a group tend to be better educated and earn more than Americans as a whole.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119] Jewish Americans also have an average of 14.7 years of schooling making them the most highly educated of all major religious groups in the United States.[^cite_note-120][^cite_note-pewforum.org-121]

Forty-four percent (55% of [Reform Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Jews)) report family incomes of over \$100,000 compared to 19% of all Americans, with the next highest group being [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) at 43%.[^cite_note-Pew_Research_Centre-122][^cite_note-123] And while 27% of Americans have a four-year university or [postgraduate education](153981.md), 59% (66% of [Reform Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Jews)) of American Jews have, the second highest of any ethnic groups after [Indian-Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian-Americans).[^cite_note-Pew_Research_Centre-122][^cite_note-Pew-2015-124][^cite_note-125] 75% of American Jews have achieved some form of [post-secondary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education) if two-year vocational and community college diplomas and certificates are also included.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-pewforum.org-121]

31% of American Jews hold a [graduate degree](153981.md); this figure is compared with the general American population where 11% of Americans hold a graduate degree.[^cite_note-Pew_Research_Centre-122] White collar professional jobs have been attractive to Jews and much of the community tend to take up professional white collar careers requiring tertiary education involving formal credentials where the respectability and reputability of professional jobs is highly prized within Jewish culture. While 46% of Americans work in [professional and managerial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-collar_worker) jobs, 61% of American Jews work as professionals, many of whom are highly educated, salaried professionals whose work is largely self-directed in [management, professional, and related occupations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profession) such as engineering, science, medicine, investment banking, finance, law, and academia.[^cite_note-129]

Much of the Jewish American community lead middle class lifestyles.[^cite_note-Frum-2010-130] While the median household net worth of the typical American family is \$99,500, among American Jews the figure is \$443,000.[^cite_note-Triple-131]<sup>: 7 </sup> [^cite_note-132] In addition, the median Jewish American income is estimated to be in the range of \$97,000 to \$98,000, nearly twice as high the American national median.[^cite_note-Triple-131]<sup>: 53 </sup> Either of these two statistics may be [confounded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confounding) by the fact that the Jewish population is on average older than other religious groups in the country, with 51% of polled adults over the age of 50 compared to 41% nationally.[^cite_note-Pew-2015-124] Older people tend to both [have higher income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_income_in_the_United_States#Income_distribution) and be more highly educated. By 2016, [Modern Orthodox Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Orthodox_Jews) had a median household income of \$158,000, while [Open Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_Orthodoxy) Jews had a median household income at \$185,000 (compared to the American median household income of \$59,000 for 2016).[^cite_note-133]

According to a 2020 study by the Pew Research Center, 23% of American Jewish living in households with incomes of at least \$200,000. [Conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Judaism) (27%) and [Reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism) (26%) are more likely to live in households with incomes of at least \$200,000 than those who are [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) (16%).[^cite_note-Pew-2021a-46] The study found also that American Jews are a comparatively well-educated group, around 60% of them are [college graduates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_graduate). [Reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism) (64%) and [Conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Judaism) (55%) are more likely to obtain college or [postgraduate education](153981.md) than those who are [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) (37%).[^cite_note-Pew-2021a-46]

As a whole, American and Canadian Jews donate more than \$9 billion a year to [charity](1176679.md). This reflects Jewish traditions of supporting social services as a way of living out the dictates of Jewish law. Most of the charities that benefit are not specifically Jewish organizations.[^cite_note-134]

While the median income of Jewish Americans is high, some Jewish communities have high levels of poverty. In the New York area, there are approximately 560,000 Jews living in poor or near-poor households, representing about 20% of the New York metropolitan Jewish community. Jewish people affected by poverty are disproportionately likely to be children, young adults, the elderly, people with low educational attainment, part-time workers, immigrants from the former Soviet Union, immigrants without American citizenship, Holocaust survivors, Orthodox families, and single adults including single parents.[^cite_note-135] [Disability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disability) is a major factor in the socioeconomic status of disabled Jews. Disabled Jews are significantly more likely to be low-income compared to able-bodied Jews, while high-income Jews are significantly less likely to be disabled.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] [Secular Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_secularism), Jews of no denomination, and people who identify as "just Jewish" are also more likely to live in poverty compared to Jews affiliated with a religious denomination.[^cite_note-138]

According to analysis by [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Gallup_Organization), American Jews have the highest [well-being](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quality_of_life) of any ethnic or religious group in America.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]

The great majority of school-age Jewish students attend public schools, although Jewish day schools and yeshivas are to be found throughout the country. [Jewish cultural studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture) and [Hebrew language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language) instruction is also commonly offered at synagogues in the form of supplementary Hebrew schools or Sunday schools.

From the early 1900s until the 1950s, [quota systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numerus_clausus) were imposed at elite colleges and universities particularly in the Northeast, as a response to the growing number of children of recent Jewish immigrants; these limited the number of Jewish students accepted, and greatly reduced their previous attendance. Jewish enrollment at Cornell's School of Medicine fell from 40% to 4% between the world wars, and Harvard's fell from 30% to 4%.[^cite_note-141] Before 1945, only a few Jewish professors were permitted as instructors at elite universities. In 1941, for example, antisemitism drove [Milton Friedman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milton_Friedman) from a non-tenured assistant professorship at the [University of Wisconsin–Madison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Wisconsin%E2%80%93Madison).[^cite_note-Friedmans-1998-142] [Harry Levin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Levin) became the first Jewish full professor in the [Harvard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University) English department in 1943, but the Economics department decided not to hire [Paul Samuelson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Samuelson) in 1948. Harvard hired its first Jewish biochemists in 1954.[^cite_note-143]

According to [Clark Kerr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark_Kerr), [Martin Meyerson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Meyerson) in 1965 became the first Jew to serve, albeit temporarily, as the leader of a major American [research university](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_university).[^cite_note-Kerr-2001-144] That year, Meyerson served as acting chancellor of the [University of California, Berkeley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_California,_Berkeley), but was unable to obtain a permanent appointment as a result of a combination of tactical errors on his part and antisemitism on the [UC Board of Regents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regents_of_the_University_of_California).[^cite_note-Kerr-2001-144] Meyerson served as the president of the [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania) from 1970 to 1981.

By 1986, a third of the presidents of the elite undergraduate [final clubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_clubs) at Harvard were Jewish.[^cite_note-Friedmans-1998-142] [Rick Levin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Levin) was president of Yale University from 1993 to 2013, [Judith Rodin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_Rodin) was president of the University of Pennsylvania from 1994 to 2004 (and is currently president of the [Rockefeller Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockefeller_Foundation)), Paul Samuelson's nephew, [Lawrence Summers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence_Summers), was president of [Harvard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University) from 2001 until 2006, and [Harold Shapiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Tafler_Shapiro) was president of [Princeton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University) from 1992 until 2000.

## Religion



[Touro Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Touro_Synagogue) (built in 1759), in Newport, Rhode Island, has the oldest still existing synagogue building in the United States.

### Observances and engagement

See also: [Religion in the United States § Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_the_United_States#Judaism)

The American Jews' majority continues to identify themselves with Judaism and its main traditions, such as [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Judaism), [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) and [Reform Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism).[^cite_note-Wertheimer1991p86–88-149][^cite_note-150] But, already in the 1980s, 20–30 percent of members of largest Jewish communities, such as of New York City, Chicago, Miami, and others, rejected a [denominational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_movements) label.[^cite_note-Wertheimer1991p86–88-149]



*[Birkat Hachama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birkat_Hachama)* of Conservative Jews, Encino, Los Angeles

According to the 1990 [National Jewish Population Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Jewish_Population_Survey), 38% of Jews were affiliated with the Reform tradition, 35% were Conservative, 6% were Orthodox, 1% were Reconstructionists, 10% linked themselves to some other tradition, and 10% said they are "just Jewish".[^cite_note-151]



US serviceman lighting a Menorah in observance of the first day of [Hanukkah](7388.md)

Jewish religious practice in America is quite varied. Among the 4.3 million American Jews described as "strongly connected" to Judaism, over 80% report some sort of active engagement with Judaism,[^cite_note-152] ranging from attending at daily prayer services on one end of the spectrum, to as little as attending only [Passover Seders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passover_Seder) or lighting [Hanukkah](7388.md) candles on the other.

According to a 2020 study by the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), 37% of American Jews identify as [Reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Judaism), 32% do not with a particular branch, 17% identify as [Conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Judaism), 9% identify as [Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism), and 4% are members of other Jewish branches, such as Reconstructionist or Humanist Judaism.[^cite_note-Pew-2021a-46] The survey found that Jews are far less religious than American adults as a whole, 8% of American Jews go to the synagogue at least once a month, 27% go few times a year, and 56% seldom or never go to the synagogue.[^cite_note-Pew-2021a-46]

A 2003 [Harris Poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harris_Poll) found that 16% of American Jews go to the synagogue at least once a month, 42% go less frequently but at least once a year, and 42% go less frequently than once a year.[^cite_note-Taylor-2003-153] The survey found that of the 4.3 million strongly connected Jews, 46% belong to a synagogue. Among those households who belong to a synagogue, 38% are members of Reform synagogues, 33% Conservative, 22% Orthodox, 2% [Reconstructionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstructionist_Judaism), and 5% other types.

Traditionally, [Sephardi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews) and [Mizrahi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jews) do not have different branches (Orthodox, Conservative, Reform, etc.) but usually remain observant and religious. However, their synagogues are generally considered Orthodox or [Sephardic Haredim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Haredim) by non-Sephardic Jews. But, not all Sephardim are Orthodox; among the pioneers of Reform Judaism movement in the 1820s there was the Sephardic congregation [Beth Elohim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahal_Kadosh_Beth_Elohim) in [Charleston, South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston,_South_Carolina).[^cite_note-154]

The survey discovered that Jews in the [Northeast](431669.md) and [Midwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwest) are generally more observant than Jews in the South or West. Reflecting a trend also observed among other religious groups, Jews in the Northwestern United States are typically the least observant.

The 2008 [American Religious Identification Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Religious_Identification_Survey) found that around 3.4 million American Jews call themselves [religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Jews)—out of a general Jewish population of about 5.4 million. The number of Jews who identify themselves as only culturally Jewish has risen from 20% in 1990 to 37% in 2008, according to the study. In the same period, the number of all US adults who said they had no religion rose from 8% to 15%. Jews are more likely to be secular than Americans in general, the researchers said. About half of all US Jews—including those who consider themselves religiously observant—claim in the survey that they have a secular worldview and see no contradiction between that outlook and their faith, according to the study's authors. Researchers attribute the trends among American Jews to the high rate of intermarriage and "disaffection from Judaism" in the United States.[^cite_note-155]

### Religious beliefs

American Jews are more likely to be [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism) or [agnostics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnosticism) than most Americans, especially when they are compared with American [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) or [Catholics](606848.md). A 2003 poll found that while 79% of Americans [believe in God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belief_in_God), only 48% of American Jews do, compared to 79% and 90% of American Catholics and Protestants respectively. While 66% of Americans said that they were "absolutely certain" of God's existence, 24% of American Jews said the same. And though 9% of Americans believe that there is [no God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism) (8% of American [Catholics](606848.md) and 4% of American [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism)), 19% of American Jews believe that God does not exist.[^cite_note-Taylor-2003-153]

A 2009 Harris Poll showed that American Jews constitute the one religious group which is most accepting of the science of [evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution), with 80% accepting evolution, compared to 51% for Catholics, 32% for Protestants, and 16% of born-again Christians.[^cite_note-156] They were also less likely to believe in supernatural phenomena such as [miracles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miracle), [angels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angel), or [heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heaven).

#### Christianity

A 2013 [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) report found that 1.7 million American Jewish adults, 1.6 million of whom were raised in Jewish homes or had Jewish ancestry, identified as [Christians](18337522.md) or [Messianic Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messianic_Judaism) but also consider themselves ethnically Jewish. Another 700,000 American Christian adults considered themselves "[Jews by affinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaizers)" or "grafted-in" Jews.[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-HowMany2013-158] According to a 2020 study by the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), 19% of those who say they were raised Jewish, consider themselves Christian.[^cite_note-159]

A 2025 Pew Research Center report found that 7% of American adults who identify ethnically as Jewish were raised as Christians. Additionally, among those who were raised as religious Jews rather than in a Christian environment, 2% now identify as Christian.[^cite_note-Lesage-2025-160]

#### Buddhism

Main article: [Jewish Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Buddhist)

[Jewish Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Buddhist)[^cite_note-Cohn-Sherbok-2010-161] are overrepresented among [American Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_the_United_States); this is specifically the case among those Jews whose parents are not [Buddhist](3267529.md), and those Jews who are without a Buddhist heritage, with between one fifth[^cite_note-162] and 30% of all American Buddhists identifying as Jewish[^cite_note-163] though only [2% of Americans are Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_the_United_States#Judaism). Nicknamed *Jubu*s, an increasing number of American Jews have started to adopt Buddhist spiritual practices, while at the same time, they are continuing to identify with and practice Judaism. It may be the individual practices both Judaism and Buddhism.[^cite_note-Cohn-Sherbok-2010-161] Notable American Jewish Buddhists include: [Robert Downey Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Downey_Jr.)[^cite_note-164] [Allen Ginsberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen_Ginsberg),[^cite_note-165] [Linda Pritzker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Pritzker),[^cite_note-166] [Jonathan F.P. Rose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_F.P._Rose),[^cite_note-167] [Goldie Hawn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goldie_Hawn)[^cite_note-168] and daughter [Kate Hudson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kate_Hudson), [Steven Seagal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Seagal), [Adam Yauch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Yauch) of the rap group [The Beastie Boys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beastie_Boys), and [Garry Shandling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garry_Shandling). Film makers the [Coen Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coen_Brothers) have been influenced by Buddhism as well for a time.[^cite_note-169]

#### Islam

A 2025 Pew Research Center report revealed that 1% of American Jewish adults who were raised in Judaism later identified as [Muslim](19541.md).[^cite_note-Lesage-2025-160]
